<a id='question-150'></a>

### Question 150: Protein Trafficking & Signal Peptide Cleavage
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of the Signal Recognition Particle (SRP) in targeting nascent polypeptide chains to the endoplasmic reticulum (ER) membrane for co-translational translocation, and the subsequent cleavage of the signal peptide by signal peptidase. (理解信号识别粒子 (SRP) 在将新合成多肽链靶向至内质网 (ER) 膜进行共翻译易位中的作用，以及信号肽酶随后切割信号肽的过程。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male presents to the emergency department with acute onset of severe abdominal pain, nausea, vomiting, and fever. He has a history of chronic pancreatitis, often exacerbated by alcohol consumption. Laboratory tests reveal elevated serum amylase and lipase levels, consistent with an acute pancreatitis flare. Further investigation reveals a novel homozygous mutation in the *SRP14* gene, which encodes a component of the Signal Recognition Particle (SRP). The mutation results in a non-functional SRP14 protein. Cellular studies on his pancreatic acinar cells show that newly synthesized pancreatic enzymes, such as trypsinogen and chymotrypsinogen, are synthesized with N-terminal signal peptides. However, these enzymes are found predominantly in the cytosol instead of being translocated into the ER lumen and subsequently secreted. Immunoblotting analysis of cell lysates reveals the presence of these enzymes in the cytosol, but they are not processed; the N-terminal signal peptides remain attached. Which of the following molecular events is most directly impaired in this patient's pancreatic acinar cells due to the defective SRP14 protein?

(A) The binding of the signal peptide to the Sec61 translocon channel.
(B) The cleavage of the signal peptide by signal peptidase within the ER lumen.
(C) The translocation of the nascent polypeptide chain through the Sec61 translocon channel.
(D) The recognition and binding of the ribosome-mRNA complex to the ER membrane.
(E) The folding and modification of the polypeptide chain within the ER lumen by chaperones.

#### Correct Answer: D

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Molecular Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 52-year-old male with acute pancreatitis flare, history of chronic pancreatitis (alcohol-related). This sets the context, involving pancreatic acinar cells which are responsible for synthesizing and secreting digestive enzymes.
- **Genetic Defect:** Homozygous mutation in *SRP14* gene, leading to non-functional SRP14 protein. SRP14 is a component of the Signal Recognition Particle (SRP).
- **Cellular Observation:** Pancreatic enzymes (trypsinogen, chymotrypsinogen) are synthesized with N-terminal signal peptides (expected for secreted proteins).
- **Localization:** These enzymes are found predominantly in the cytosol, not the ER lumen (where they should be for secretion).
- **Processing:** The signal peptides remain attached to the enzymes, indicating they are not cleaved.
- **Question:** What molecular event is *most directly* impaired due to the defective SRP14?

The key clues are the defective SRP14, the presence of signal peptides on proteins in the cytosol, and the failure of these proteins to reach the ER lumen. This points directly to a problem in the initial targeting step.

##### 2. Biochemical, Molecular & Genetic Deep-Dive (生化代谢、基因靶点与分子机制深度剖析)
- **Protein Secretion Pathway:** Secreted proteins (like pancreatic enzymes) are synthesized on ribosomes.
- **Signal Peptide:** These proteins have an N-terminal signal peptide that directs them to the ER.
- **SRP Function:** As the signal peptide emerges from the ribosome, it is recognized and bound by the Signal Recognition Particle (SRP). SRP consists of SRP RNA and several SRP proteins, including SRP14.
- **SRP-Ribosome-mRNA Complex:** The binding of SRP to the signal peptide and the ribosome pauses translation. The entire complex (SRP, ribosome, mRNA, nascent polypeptide) is then targeted to the ER membrane.
- **Sec61 Interaction:** SRP binds to the Sec61 translocon channel on the ER membrane.
- **Translocation:** The ribosome docks onto the Sec61 translocon, translation resumes, and the nascent polypeptide chain is threaded through the Sec61 channel into the ER lumen (co-translational translocation).
- **Signal Peptidase:** Once inside the ER lumen, the signal peptide is cleaved off by the enzyme signal peptidase.
- **SRP Release:** After the polypeptide chain enters the ER lumen, SRP dissociates from the Sec61 complex, allowing translation to continue.
- **Patient's Defect:** The patient has a non-functional SRP14 protein. This means the SRP particle cannot form correctly or function properly.
- **Consequence:** Without functional SRP, the signal peptide on the nascent polypeptide chain cannot be recognized. Therefore, the ribosome-mRNA complex carrying the nascent protein cannot be targeted to the ER membrane.
- **Result:** The protein remains in the cytosol, translation continues, but it never enters the ER lumen, and the signal peptide is not cleaved.

Therefore, the most direct impairment is the recognition and binding of the ribosome-mRNA complex to the ER membrane, which is mediated by SRP.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) The binding of the signal peptide to the Sec61 translocon channel. This is incorrect. The signal peptide binds to SRP, not directly to Sec61. SRP then binds to Sec61. The primary defect is the SRP's inability to bind the signal peptide and target the complex to the ER.
- (B) The cleavage of the signal peptide by signal peptidase within the ER lumen. This is incorrect. Signal peptidase acts *after* the protein has been translocated into the ER lumen. Since the protein never reaches the ER lumen in this patient, signal peptidase cannot act on it. The signal peptide remains attached because translocation never occurs.
- (C) The translocation of the nascent polypeptide chain through the Sec61 translocon channel. This is incorrect. Translocation requires the ribosome to be correctly positioned at the Sec61 channel, which is dependent on SRP binding to both the signal peptide and Sec61. While translocation is ultimately impaired, the *most direct* defect caused by non-functional SRP is the failure to target the ribosome-mRNA complex to the ER membrane in the first place. Translocation cannot happen if the complex never reaches the translocon.
- (E) The folding and modification of the polypeptide chain within the ER lumen by chaperones. This is incorrect. Folding and modification occur *after* the protein has been translocated into the ER lumen and the signal peptide has been cleaved. Since the protein remains in the cytosol and is not processed, these events do not occur.

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The primary function of SRP is to recognize the signal sequence on nascent polypeptides and target the ribosome-mRNA complex to the ER membrane for co-translational translocation. A defect in SRP prevents this initial targeting step. Key differentials include defects in Sec61 (translocation channel), signal peptidase (signal peptide cleavage), or ER chaperones (protein folding). Understanding the sequence of events in protein secretion is crucial.
